● Since my handoff commit (4cd7850), 23 more commits landed on
  app-v2-accounts-ai-agents, all authored as DevHusnainAi between Sep 28 21:59
  and Sep 29 03:14. Together they change 73 files (+3227 / −532 lines). Your
  local branch matches origin, and CI passed on the three latest commits
  (a78c146, 085be66, 8f1df48).

  Fix with AI and the incident flow
  - b14a40e An AI patch can no longer touch CI config or the replay files.
  - 9893331 Flow polish: a sample incident, a recovery moment, a report snippet
    and a proof step.
  - c3e51f2 Paging is more careful: one reported 500 is held and a second within
    a minute pages. Also added: self-heal after 10 quiet minutes, dismissing an
    incident, and retrying a failed proof.
  - eaf6d07 Audit fixes: reported errors must be confirmed, code fixes are no
    longer offered when there's nothing to replay, and proofs are pinned.
  - 085be66 An incident detail screen: cause, failing requests, suggested fix,
    the AI's second opinion, and a timeline.

  AI provider
  - 3f76254 / efb6f8b The model is now swappable. The default is NVIDIA Nemotron
    3 Super (on the eval: 100% of its picks were allowed fixes, 93% were
    correct). Claude on Vertex is still an option.
  - ec130ea / 306652a Added retries and an optional Groq-style backup, and the
    eval can score any OpenAI-compatible provider. The deterministic rules still
    decide the suggested fix.
  - 4e51b30 The model's opinion is saved on each incident in context.ai.

  Demo
  - 2f32132 / ef483e4 The demo now runs on one GCP VM with auto-deploy. The VM
    fix is now called a "reboot", with a note that disk data stays.
  - bbce292 The demo repo gets CI/CD: GitHub Actions tests main, then deploys
    over a deploy-only SSH key.
  - 8f1df48 The proof workflow passes PRs that have nothing to replay, and the
    demo app now has npm test.

  Integrations and RevenueCat
  - 7226aff One free outage, a fallback to the saved webhook copy when
    RevenueCat is down, and account deletion.
  - c4a22f6 Discord and Slack alerts, Sentry as an input, and a Railway
    provider. Sentry was the 4th roadmap item you'd left "to decide", so it's
    now partly built.
  - a9cc7de / f628fa5 "Revenue at risk" shows what each outage costs, read from
    the user's own RevenueCat, with the project detected from the key.
  - 8c82e7c New positioning: "AI writes your fixes, OpsSwipe proves them, and
    shows the cost."

  Connecting accounts
  - 51f016e / 29746c2 GitHub and Google now finish connecting on the server, so
    losing the link back to the phone loses nothing. Connect starts at GitHub's
    authorize page, so it works when the app is already installed.

  Docs
  - d3efcc4 Share, stats, and opening the app from a push tap. It also removed 
    docs/HANDOFF.md from the repo. CLAUDE.md now points to a private copy at
    ../opsswipe-private/HANDOFF.md, and that folder isn't on this machine. If
    you're on the new computer, copy it over privately; otherwise the next agent
    has no secrets inventory.
  - a78c146 Updated CLAUDE.md to match all of the above.

  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/synthetic-watching-canyon.md · /plan to edit
     Plan: prove the revert loop, one-click Slack/Discord, and the two wedges

     Context

     - Deadline: Oct 1, 11:45am PKT. The Supabase auth toggles are done. Three
       asks, in the order we'll do them:
       a. Test a real failure end to end: OpsSwipe reverts it with a PR, CI
          proves the fix, we merge, the service comes back up.
          - Why it hasn't worked yet: the demo's bad release (RELEASE_OK = 
            false) breaks /. The demo repo's own unit tests check /, so CI
            failed on a91013f "Ship new homepage" and never deployed. The VM
            still answers 200, and demo main sits on an undeployed bad commit.
          - Real outages are bugs the tests missed, so the demo bug must be one
            too.
       b. One-click Slack and Discord alerts, with no webhook for the user to
          create.
          - Both platforms' OAuth returns a webhook for the channel the user
            picks: Slack's incoming-webhook scope, Discord's webhook.incoming
            scope.
          - So _shared/alerts.ts (sending) stays unchanged; only the connect
            flow changes.
          - You chose alerts only, no approve buttons in chat.
       c. Wedge: you picked both
          - (a) the approval layer for AI agents: agents ask your phone before
            touching production;
          - (b) the pager for indie app devs.
          - Both are sharpened by adding to what exists, not by a rebuild.

     Step 1: Make the demo bug slip past tests, then run Outage 2 (first, ~1h)

     Demo code. Change it in both ../opsswipe-demo-target/server.js and its
     mirror infra/demo-web/server.js (currently identical apart from the flag):
     - Add GET /api/price → {"plan":"pro","price":9}. The homepage's inline
       script fetches it, so every visitor exercises it.
     - The bad release breaks only /api/price: if (!RELEASE_OK && req.url === 
       '/api/price') return fail(req, res, 500, …).
     - / stays 200, so the tests still pass and CI deploys the bug, like a real
       untested path.
     - Set RELEASE_OK = true on demo main in that same commit. This heals the
       stuck undeployed bad commit.

     Chaos script (infra/chaos.sh):
     - release: after the push, wait for the deploy (poll /api/price until it
       returns 500, up to about 3 min).
     - Then send a few requests 20s apart. Two reports within 60s open the
       incident; this uses the existing confirmsReport logic.
     - heal is unchanged.

     Run it with you on the phone. Watch each stage:
     1. The chaos release deploys and the card appears within ~1–2 min,
        suggesting Revert PR (Fix with AI is also offered).
     2. The incident detail shows the cause and the AI's opinion.
     3. Swipe and fingerprint: a PR opens in the demo repo with
        .opsswipe/replays/*.json.
     4. The opsswipe-proof workflow replays GET /api/price, and the proof posts
        to proof/.
     5. The card offers Merge. Swipe: the PR merges and deploy.yml redeploys.
     6. The incident resolves (afterFix('merge_pr')) and the "back up" push
        arrives.

     Then chaos.sh heal, and a second run with Fix with AI (the NVIDIA patch
     goes through the same proof).

     Before the run you log in to gcloud, supabase and eas. We'll watch the
     function logs if a stage stalls.

     Step 1b: Fix with AI, answering: how the AI changes code, the PR structure,
     where you see the diff, and tests (~3h)

     How it works today (execute/index.ts aiFixPr + _shared/patch.ts):
     1. Pick the bad commit. OpsSwipe takes the commit that broke production:
        the reported release sha, Render's deploy, or else the branch head.
     2. Give the model context. It gets the commit message, its diff, the full
        current content of every file that commit touched, the symptom, and the
        failing production requests.
     3. The model writes whole files. It returns complete new file contents,
        plus a 1–2 sentence summary. This runs on NVIDIA Nemotron (Groq backup)
        via llmPatcher, or Claude via vertexPatcher.
     4. checkPatch filters the answer. Only files the bad commit touched
        survive. Never .github/ or .opsswipe/. Real changes only, each file ≤ 60
        KB. An empty result means the model declined.
     5. OpsSwipe opens the PR, committing through GitHub's Git Data API with no
        clone:
        - Branch: opsswipe/fix-<sha7>-<ts>
        - Title: Fix <incident> after <sha7>
        - Body: incident, symptom, "Broke in: <sha7> <commit message>", the AI's
          summary, and how many failing requests CI will replay
        - Files: the patched files, plus .opsswipe/replays/<sha7>.json (the
          scrubbed failing requests)
     6. The proof runs. The opsswipe-proof workflow runs the repo's tests,
        starts the PR build, replays every saved request, and posts the result
        to proof/ with an OIDC token.
     7. Merge is gated on the proof. It unlocks only when the proof is for the
        PR's current head sha.

     Gaps: you only see the diff by tapping through to GitHub, and the model is
     told not to write tests.

     Change 1: see the diff in the app, before you merge
     - connect: new action pr_diff {incidentId}, owner-checked. It calls GitHub
       GET /repos/{repo}/pulls/{n}/files with the installation token, which
       already exists in _shared/github.ts. It returns [{file, status, 
       additions, deletions, patch}], capped at about 20 KB total.
     - IncidentDetail.tsx: new "Changes" section. Each file gets a header
       server.js  +1 −1, then its hunks in monospace, with added lines green and
       removed lines red.
     - Scope: covers revert PRs too.
     - The Merge card: shows a one-line summary, "1 file, +1 −1 · proof 3/3
       passed", and tapping it opens the detail. So you've seen what you're
       merging before you swipe. The biometric merge is the review.

     Change 2: the AI writes a regression test ("evaluation") with every fix
     - Prompt (patch.ts SYSTEM): "also add ONE regression test that sends the
       failing request(s) and asserts success, in the repo's existing test
       style."
     - Input: one existing test file is added as a style example. It's found
       with the Git trees API (GET /git/trees/<branch>?recursive=1, first path
       matching the test regex), a small helper in github.ts.
     - checkPatch: also allows new files, but only test files (test/, tests/,
       __tests__/, *.test.*, *.spec.*). Still never .github/ or .opsswipe/, and
       still capped in size.
     - Schema: the zod schema and the llmPatcher shape accept that one extra
       path.
     - The proof then means two checks: the repo's tests pass (now including the
       AI's new test), and every replayed production request passes.
     - PR body: gets a "How this was proven" section listing the regression test
       file and the replay count.
     - Tests: patch.test.ts checks that a new test file is allowed while a new
       non-test file, or anything under .github/, is rejected.

     Stretch (skipped unless time allows):
     - A "fails before the fix" check: CI runs the new test against the bad
       commit and expects it to fail, which proves the test really catches the
       bug.
     - A small patch eval: 3–5 bug cases in evals/, scoring whether the model's
       patch makes the failing request pass.

     Step 2: One-click "Add to Slack" / "Add to Discord" (~3h, including the 
     dev-portal setup)

     Reuse the GitHub/Google server-side OAuth pattern:
     - _shared/oauthState.ts: newState, takeState, and a complete* function.
     - oauth-callback/index.ts dispatches on pending.kind.

     Migration. Add oauth_states.kind values 'slack' and 'discord', via drop and
     re-add of the check constraint, as 20260929020000_oauth_states.sql did.

     _shared/oauthState.ts: completeAlerts(owner, kind, code)
     - Slack: POST https://slack.com/api/oauth.v2.access with the client id,
       secret, code and redirect_uri → incoming_webhook.url and .channel.
     - Discord: POST https://discord.com/api/oauth2/token with
       grant_type=authorization_code, the code and redirect_uri, plus basic auth
       → webhook.url, and the channel via webhook.channel_id/name.
     - Then call a new shared saveAlerts(owner, url, label), lifted out of
       connect set_alerts. It sends the test message, stores the URL in Vault,
       upserts connections(kind 'alerts', account '<slack|discord> · #channel'),
       and deletes the old secret.
     - alertKind() still validates the host, so an OAuth reply can't point the
       server at an arbitrary URL.

     connect/index.ts
     - New alerts_start {kind} returns the authorize URL:
       - Slack: https://slack.com/oauth/v2/authorize?scope=incoming-webhook…
       - Discord: https://discord.com/oauth2/authorize?response_type=code&scope=
         webhook.incoming…
       - Both use state=newState(owner, kind) plus the Expo Go return suffix,
         the same as github_start/gcp_start.
     - set_alerts stays only for disconnect (an empty URL).
     - status returns the channel label.

     oauth-callback/index.ts: else if (pending.kind === 'slack' || 'discord') 
     await completeAlerts(…), then done=<kind>.

     App app/src/Settings.tsx
     - Replace the webhook URL field with two buttons, "Add to Slack" and "Add
       to Discord".
     - They open the URL the same way Connect GitHub does, then refresh on
       return (the AppState refresh already exists).
     - When connected: "Posting to Slack · #incidents" plus "Stop posting
       there".

     Secrets: SLACK_CLIENT_ID/SECRET and DISCORD_CLIENT_ID/SECRET.

     What you do (I'll guide you):
     - Slack: create an app at api.slack.com, enable Incoming Webhooks, set the
       redirect URL to …/functions/v1/oauth-callback, and turn on public
       distribution so other workspaces can add it (no review needed).
     - Discord: create an app in the developer portal and add the OAuth2
       redirect.
     - Store it privately: add the IDs to opsswipe-private.

     Tests (supabase/functions/tests/): the authorize URL shape; completeAlerts
     with a mocked fetch for both providers, using the existing mockFetch
     pattern in providers.test.ts; and rejecting a non-Slack/Discord webhook URL
     coming back from OAuth.

     Step 3: Wedge (a), approval layer for AI agents (~3–4h)

     Today agents can only propose OpsSwipe's own fixes on a connected service.
     The wedge is any risky action an agent wants to take: git push --force,
     supabase db reset, terraform apply, a prod migration. The agent asks, your
     phone buzzes, you fingerprint, and the agent proceeds. The agent still runs
     the action itself; OpsSwipe is the gate.

     _shared/agents.ts parseProposal:
     - A body without service but with command (up to 500 chars) and reason
       becomes an approval: action: 'approve'.
     - Service fixes behave as today.

     agent/index.ts:
     - Insert the incident with service_id: null. The unique index is on
       service_id, and nulls don't collide.
     - target_server is the agent's name, actions ['approve'], and
       context.agent.command.
     - Polling is unchanged: proposalStatus already returns approved/declined.

     execute/index.ts:
     - approve is valid only when inc.suggested_by === 'agent' and inc.actions
       includes it.
     - It skips the service/actionsFor check and runs nothing: audit executed
       "approved", and the incident resolves.
     - Metering reuses the free-outage meter as-is: the first approval is free,
       then the paywall. That's a Pro feature with no new code.

     Types and app:
     - Add 'approve' to Action in targets.ts.
     - app/src/fixes.ts: add an approve entry ("Swipe to approve").
     - SwipeCard.tsx: show context.agent.command in monospace.

     Claude Code hook: new integrations/claude-code/opsswipe-gate.sh, about 30
     lines of bash + curl + jq.
     - It's a PreToolUse hook on Bash. Risky commands (a regex list: push 
       --force|db reset|drop |terraform apply|kubectl delete|rm -rf /…) go to
       POST /agent with an ops_ token, then it polls GET /agent?id= every 3s.
     - Approved → exit 0. Declined or timeout → exit 2 with a reason, which
       Claude Code shows to the model.
     - Timeout 600s in the hook config.
     - The setup snippet goes in docs/SETUP.md. This is the demo moment: Claude
       Code tries a force-push, and the phone asks.

     Stretch, only if time allows: integrations/mcp/opsswipe-mcp.js, a
     dependency-free stdio MCP server (JSON-RPC by hand, ~60 lines) with one
     request_approval tool, so Cursor and Claude Desktop can use it too.

     Tests: agents.test.ts covers parsing a command approval; a flow/execute
     unit test covers approve resolving with no provider call.

     Step 4: Wedge (b), pager for indie app devs (~2h, mostly words)

     Positioning in README.md, docs/SUBMISSION.md and the onboarding welcome
     slides (app/src/Onboarding.tsx):

     ▎ Your app broke at 2am. Fix it from bed, see what it's costing you, and 
     ▎ let your AI agents ask before they touch prod.

     This leads with Revenue at risk (already built), Railway/Render/VM, and
     cheap Pro.

     Paywall/Settings copy: "First outage free, then Pro."

     Stretch: a Vercel provider (rollback via the Vercel API; the commit from
     the deployment feeds revert/AI fix). It's skipped unless steps 1–3 finish
     early.

     Step 5: Ship

     1. Run deno task test, check, lint, fmt --check and eval; the app's
        typecheck, lint and test; and the SQL tests.
     2. supabase db push, deploy the functions (connect, oauth-callback, agent,
        execute), and set the new secrets.
     3. Commit to app-v2-accounts-ai-agents and push, with CI green.
     4. Update the docs:
        - CLAUDE.md, docs/SETUP.md and docs/DECISIONS.md in the repo;
        - the Master plan, plus a new "Plan v6" tab in the Claude Docs doc;
        - HANDOFF.md in opsswipe-private.

     Verification

     - Step 1: the full Outage 2 loop on the phone, as listed above: the PR, a
       green proof run, the merge, the redeploy, /api/price returns 200 again,
       and the incident resolved in the app.
     - Step 1b: on the second Outage 2 run, choose Fix with AI and check:
       - The PR contains the one-line fix in server.js and
         test/api-price.test.js, or similar.
       - The incident detail shows both diffs.
       - The proof shows the tests passing (the new one included) and every
         replayed request passing.
       - The Merge card shows "+N −M · proof k/k".
     - Step 2: tap Add to Slack → pick a channel → a test message lands →
       trigger chaos.sh gcp → the incident posts to Slack. Repeat for Discord.
     - Step 3: configure the hook in a scratch repo and ask Claude Code to
       force-push.
       - The card appears on the phone. Decline, and Claude is blocked with the
         reason.
       - Approve, and the command runs.
       - GET /agent shows approved.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● The new code is live: connect and execute are deployed, so the diff view and
  the AI regression test work now. No revert PR yet, and the VM still returns
  500 on /api/price. So the card is still waiting on your phone: tap it to see
  the cause, then swipe Revert PR.

  Does the app say why it failed and how to prevent it?

  It only answers half of that today.
  - Why it failed: yes. The incident detail shows the release that was running
    when it broke (commit c1a2b3d "Ship new pricing"), the failing requests (GET
    /api/price → 500), the reason the rules gave, and the AI's second opinion.
  - How to prevent it: only partly. After today's change, a Fix with AI PR adds
    a regression test, so that exact bug can't come back. But nothing tells you
    in plain words "this happened because X; to prevent it, do Y."

  What I'd add is a short postmortem card written automatically once an incident
  resolves. It would show in the incident detail and in "Share what happened":
  - What happened: the symptom, the bad commit, how long it was down and what it
    cost (Revenue at risk).
  - Why: the root cause, from the bad commit's diff and the failing requests
    (the AI already reads both).
  - Prevent it next time: 2–3 concrete steps. For this demo: "/api/price had no
    test; the regression test in PR #N now covers it", "add a smoke test that
    hits every API route after deploy", "deploy behind a health check on
    /api/price, not just /".

  It's about 2 hours: one AI call when the incident resolves, stored on the
  incident, plus one section on the detail screen. It also helps the indie-dev
  wedge, because it's the part people forward to their team.

  A virus/worm scanner on the VM?

  I'd skip antivirus. On a Linux server it catches very little: worms get in
  through unpatched software, weak SSH and exposed ports, not infected files.
  What actually protects the VM:
  1. Automatic security updates: turn on unattended-upgrades, which patches the
     OS every night. This is the big one.
  2. SSH keys only, with password login off. Also block project-wide SSH keys:
     your key currently reaches voxapk-prod too.
  3. Firewall: only ports 80/443 open to the world, and SSH only from GitHub's
     deploy.
  4. Dependency scanning: turn on Dependabot and GitHub code scanning on the
     demo repo. These catch vulnerable npm packages before they deploy.

  Items 1–3 are about 15 minutes of gcloud commands, and item 4 is a GitHub
  setting.

  As an OpsSwipe feature, security fits better as an incident source than a
  scanner. For example, "Dependabot found a critical vulnerability in express"
  becomes a card, and you swipe to merge the fix PR. That reuses the whole
  PR/proof/merge flow. It's a post-hackathon roadmap item, not something to
  build before Oct 1.

  Should I:
  1. Build the postmortem card (after this Outage 2 run and the Fix with AI
     run)?
  2. Harden the demo VM now (items 1–3, plus Dependabot on the demo repo)?

  Then we'd continue with Slack/Discord one-click.

● Clean. Running the full checks, then deploying and committing.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both are done and deployed, and everything is pushed as 179d2d7 on the branch.
  All checks pass: 74 backend tests, 7 app tests, lint and typecheck.

  1. Postmortem card

  When an incident resolves, its detail screen now shows two new parts:
  - Why it happened: 2–3 sentences on the root cause. The AI reads the symptom,
    the failing requests, the bad commit's diff and the fixes that ran, and
    names the file or route when it can see it. It's told never to invent
    details.
  - Prevent it next time: 2–4 concrete steps, such as "add a test for
    /api/price" or "run a smoke check after each deploy". If the AI fix added a
    regression test, that's listed first.

  It's written once, on the first open, and then kept on the incident. It only
  works when AI is set to NVIDIA, which it is on the server now.

  2. Demo VM security

  - Already in place: automatic nightly security updates, SSH with keys only,
    root login off, and only ports 80 and 22 open. The deploy key can only run
    the deploy script.
  - Fixed: the app was running as root. It now runs as a throwaway user that can
    only use port 80, sees the disk as read-only and can't gain more rights. I
    checked that it still serves requests and still reports its commit to
    OpsSwipe.
  - GitHub scanning on the demo repo is now on:
    - Dependabot alerts, plus automatic PRs that fix vulnerable packages
    - secret scanning, with push protection that blocks pushes containing a key
    - CodeQL code scanning

  Two things for you

  1. To see the new screens, restart the app's dev server. The backend is live,
     but the phone runs the app's code from your computer:
  ! cd app && npx expo start --dev-client --tunnel
  2. Your SSH key reaches voxapk-prod. Connecting to the demo VM from this new
     computer added your SSH key to project-wide settings. I left voxapk
     untouched, as agreed. When convenient, block project keys on it:
     gcloud compute instances add-metadata voxapk-prod --zone us-central1-a 
     --metadata block-project-ssh-keys=TRUE

  Outage 2 status

  The card is still waiting: no revert PR yet, and /api/price still returns 500.
  Once the dev server is running:
  1. Swipe Revert PR.
  2. When the proof passes, open the card and read the diff.
  3. Swipe Merge.
  4. Open the resolved incident to see the postmortem.

  Then we'll do the Fix with AI run and move on to Slack/Discord.

● Pick From a manifest. It sets everything up in one paste.

  1. From a manifest → pick the workspace you'll use for the demo → switch to
     the YAML tab and paste:

  display_information:
    name: OpsSwipe
    description: Incidents, recoveries and proven fixes from OpsSwipe, posted to
  your channel.
    background_color: "#0a0a0a"
  oauth_config:
    redirect_urls:
      - https://mwkwhfqcgxossweqwibt.supabase.co/functions/v1/oauth-callback
    scopes:
      bot:
        - incoming-webhook
  settings:
    org_deploy_enabled: false
    socket_mode_enabled: false
    token_rotation_enabled: false

  2. Next → Create.
  3. In the left menu, Manage Distribution: tick the checklist items and click
     Activate Public Distribution. This lets other people's workspaces use "Add
     to Slack", not just yours. It needs no Slack review.
  4. Basic Information → App Credentials: copy the Client ID and Client Secret.
  5. Optional: under Basic Information → Display Information, upload the
     OpsSwipe icon (app/assets/icon.png).

  Don't paste the secret here. Set it yourself (you can fill the Discord values
  in later):
  ! npx supabase secrets set SLACK_CLIENT_ID=... SLACK_CLIENT_SECRET=...

❯ it's saying oauth requires both_user                                          

● Slack wants a bot user defined whenever a bot scope is requested. Use this
  manifest instead: it's the same as before plus a features.bot_user block.

  display_information:
    name: OpsSwipe
    description: Incidents, recoveries and proven fixes from OpsSwipe, posted to
  your channel.
    background_color: "#0a0a0a"
  features:
    bot_user:
      display_name: OpsSwipe
      always_online: false
  oauth_config:
    redirect_urls:
      - https://mwkwhfqcgxossweqwibt.supabase.co/functions/v1/oauth-callback
    scopes:
      bot:
        - incoming-webhook
  settings:
    org_deploy_enabled: false
    socket_mode_enabled: false
    token_rotation_enabled: false

  Then continue with steps 2–4 from before: Create, activate public
  distribution, and copy the Client ID and Secret.

❯ do i need to create a new workspace                                           

● No new workspace is needed: any Slack workspace you're in works.

  - The workspace you pick is only where the app "lives" and where you manage
    it.

    anyone can tap "Add to Slack" and choose their own workspace and channel.

    you own. If you're only a member of your company's workspace and its admins

  Create a new one only if you have no workspace you control, or you'd like a
  clean one for the video (for example "OpsSwipe Demo" with an #incidents
  channel). That takes about a minute at slack.com/get-started.

  ⎿  [Image #8]

● Slack's mobile sign-in did this. The in-app browser wasn't signed in to Slack,
  so after you picked the workspace, Slack signed you in and then showed its
  "Get the Slack app" page. That page throws away the "Add to Slack" request.
  OpsSwipe never received anything, so nothing broke on our side.

  The page shows your sign-in details, which means the browser is now signed in 
  to Slack. Close that page and tap Add to Slack again. This time it should go
  straight to "Where should OpsSwipe post?"

  Other users will hit the same thing, so I'm making the app explain it instead
  of failing silently.

● Yes, I've now read Slack's OAuth docs. They explain the loop you're stuck in:

  ▎ "If the user is not signed in yet, the user will be asked to specify a 
  ▎ workspace to sign in to."
  ▎ — Installing with OAuth

  So "Add to Slack" only works if the browser is already signed in to Slack. On
  your phone, you're signed in to the Slack app, not the browser. When you pick
  the workspace, Slack's phone sign-in hands off to the Slack app ("Get the
  Slack app / Open Slack") instead of signing the browser in. The browser stays
  signed out, so every retry starts over at "which workspace". The docs say
  nothing about phones, and I found no setting on our side that changes this.
  The team parameter only helps once you're already signed in. That's why
  retrying and restarting Expo didn't change anything.

  Ways that actually work:
  1. Sign in to Slack in the browser itself. When Slack's page opens, tap ⋮ → 
     Desktop site, then sign in with email and the code Slack emails you (not
     "Open Slack"). The browser then has its own session, and the channel picker
     appears. Tapping "Add to Slack" again after that goes straight to the
     picker.
  2. Finish on a computer, where Slack in the browser is normal. I was adding an
     "Add to Slack from a computer" button: the phone shares the link, you open
     it on your laptop and pick a channel, and the server stores it, so the
     phone shows "Posting to Slack" when you return. You interrupted that edit,
     so it isn't in. Most apps that connect to Slack from a phone do something
     similar.
  3. Discord isn't affected. Its page works in phone browsers, so Add to Discord
     should connect in one go.

  For the demo video, I'd show Discord on the phone, or Slack using option 1.

  first?

❯ give me command to start and first tell me what is agent access in settings   

● Agent access (Settings) is where you let AI agents ask before they fix
  something. It's the "approval layer for AI agents".

  - You create a token there (starts with ops_) and give it to an agent: an AI
    SRE tool, a script, or a CI job.
  - The agent calls OpsSwipe's Approval API: "restart opsswipe-demo, because the
    error rate spiked".
  - That arrives on your phone as a card marked with the agent's name and its
    reason.
  - Nothing runs until you swipe and confirm with your fingerprint, or you
    decline. The agent can check the result.
  - Agents can only propose fixes the service already allows. They can never
    merge a code fix, since that still needs the CI proof.

  Next in the plan: letting agents ask about any risky action, not just
  OpsSwipe's own fixes, plus a Claude Code hook. For example, Claude Code tries
  git push --force, your phone buzzes, and you approve or decline.

  Commands. Run the build first. It's needed because the new in-app Slack
  sign-in uses a native component your current dev build doesn't have, and it
  takes 15 min to 2 h in EAS's free queue:
  ! cd app && npx eas-cli build --profile development --platform android
  When it finishes, install it from the link EAS prints (or
  https://expo.dev/accounts/devhusnainai/projects/opsswipe/builds). Then start
  the dev server:
  ! cd app && npx expo start --dev-client --tunnel
  Until the new build is installed, Discord, the diff view and the postmortem
  all work on your current build. Only Add to Slack needs the new one.

  What's missing for people to pay

  Today OpsSwipe fixes outages very well, but it only proves its value while
  something is on fire. Paying customers want value every week, plus the "team"
  and "trust" features. Ranked by how much they help win and get paid:

  #: 1
  Feature: Public status page, updated automatically by incidents
  (status.opsswipe.app/you)
  Who pays: Indie + B2B
  Why it wins: Indie devs already pay $20–$100/mo for status pages (Instatus,
  Better Stack). It's visible, shareable, easy to demo, and a natural Pro
  feature
  Effort: ~3 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Feature: Approval gate for AI agents: a Claude Code hook plus any-command
  approvals
  Who pays: B2B
  Why it wins: The 2026 story: agents touch prod, and a human approves from
  their
  phone. The "wow" moment in the video
  Effort: ~3–4 h (already planned)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Feature: Escalation: nobody swipes in 5 min → page a teammate (invite by
  email)
  Who pays: B2B
  Why it wins: The core of PagerDuty and incident.io, and the reason teams pay
  per seat
  Effort: ~4 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Feature: Weekly report to Discord/Slack: uptime, time to fix, "$X of revenue
  saved"
  Who pays: Indie
  Why it wins: Shows value when nothing broke, so people keep paying. Reuses
  Revenue at risk
  Effort: ~2 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Feature: One-line install for Express/Next.js (app.use(opsswipe())) instead of

  copying a snippet
  Who pays: Indie
  Why it wins: Setup drops from ~10 min to 1
  Effort: ~2 h
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: —
  Feature: Vercel/Fly providers, audit-log export, two-person approval
  Who pays: B2B
  Why it wins: Real, but after the hackathon
  Effort: —

  Proposed pricing, set in RevenueCat:
  - Free: 1 service, first outage free.
  - Indie, $9/mo: unlimited fixes, AI fixes, status page, weekly report.
  - Team, $29 per seat: escalation, agent approvals with rules, audit export.

  The jump to a paid plan maps onto real value, which matters because using
  RevenueCat thoughtfully is one of the judging criteria.

       4 +BG, BACK, EDGE, FRONT, GREEN = (10,10,10,255), (39,39,42,255),

       6 +def card(size, fill, outline=None, width=0, angle=0, dot=False)

      11 +    d.rounded_rectangle([4*SS, 4*SS, 4*SS + w, 4*SS + h], radiu

      12 +    if dot:                                                    
      13 +        s = int(w * 0.17); x = 4*SS + int(w * 0.14); y = 4*SS +
         + int(w * 0.14)                                                 
      14 +        d.ellipse([x, y, x + s, y + s], fill=GREEN)            
      15 +    return im.rotate(angle, resample=Image.BICUBIC, expand=True
         +)                                                              
      16 +                                                               
      17 +def mark(px, mono=False):                                      
      18 +    """The two cards on a transparent px*px canvas, filling ~10
         +0% of it."""                                                   
      19 +    S = px * SS                                                
      20 +    im = Image.new('RGBA', (S, S), (0,0,0,0))                  
      21 +    w, h = int(S * 0.50), int(S * 0.66)                        
      22 +    back = card((w, h), (255,255,255,255) if mono else BACK, No
         +ne if mono else EDGE, 0 if mono else int(S*0.018), 10)         
      23 +    front = card((w, h), (255,255,255,255) if mono else FRONT, 
         +angle=-12, dot=not mono)                                       
      24 +    im.alpha_composite(back, (int(S*0.30) - back.width//2, S//2
         + - back.height//2 - int(S*0.01)))                              
      25 +    im.alpha_composite(front, (int(S*0.60) - front.width//2, S/
         +/2 - front.height//2 + int(S*0.02)))                           
      26 +    bbox = im.getchannel('A').getbbox()                        
      27 +    im = im.crop(bbox)                                         
      28 +    side = max(im.size); sq = Image.new('RGBA', (side, side), (
         +0,0,0,0))                                                      
      29 +    sq.alpha_composite(im, ((side - im.width)//2, (side - im.he
         +ight)//2))                                                     
      30 +    return sq                                                  
      31 +                                                               
      32 +def place(canvas_px, fill_ratio, bg=None, mono=False):         
      33 +    S = canvas_px * SS                                         
      34 +    out = Image.new('RGBA', (S, S), bg or (0,0,0,0))           
      35 +    m = mark(canvas_px, mono).resize((int(S*fill_ratio),)*2, Im
         +age.LANCZOS)                                                   
      36 +    out.alpha_composite(m, ((S - m.width)//2, (S - m.height)//2
         +))                                                             
      37 +    return out.resize((canvas_px, canvas_px), Image.LANCZOS)   
      38 +                                                               
      39 +place(1024, 0.62, BG).save('assets/icon.png')                  
         +        # iOS / store: no mask safe zone issues                
      40 +place(512, 0.58, None).save('assets/android-icon-foreground.png
         +')       # adaptive: inside the 66dp safe zone

       6 +#   OPSSWIPE_GATE         extended regex of commands that need 
         +approval (default below)                                       
       7 +# Exit 0 lets the command run; exit 2 blocks it and tells Claud
         +e why.                                                         
       8 +set -euo pipefail                                              
       9 +URL="${OPSSWIPE_URL:-https://mwkwhfqcgxossweqwibt.supabase.co}/
         +functions/v1/agent"                                            
      10 +GATE="${OPSSWIPE_GATE:-git push.*(--force|-f\b)|git reset --har
         +d|db (reset|push)|drop (table|database)|terraform (apply|destro
         +y)|kubectl (delete|apply)|rm -rf /|DELETE FROM|npm publish|gclo
         +ud .* delete|fly (deploy|destroy)|vercel .*--prod}"            
      11 +                                                               
      12 +cmd=$(jq -r '.tool_input.command // empty')                    
      13 +[ -n "$cmd" ] && echo "$cmd" | grep -Eiq "$GATE" || exit 0     
      14 +: "${OPSSWIPE_AGENT_TOKEN:?set OPSSWIPE_AGENT_TOKEN (OpsSwipe: 
         +Settings -> Agent access)}"                                    
      15 +auth=(-H "Authorization: Bearer $OPSSWIPE_AGENT_TOKEN")        
      16 +                                                               
      17 +body=$(jq -n --arg c "$cmd" --arg d "$PWD" '{command: $c, title
         +: "Claude Code wants to run a command", reason: ("In " + $d + "
         +. Approve to let it run.")}')                                  
      18 +id=$(curl -fsS "${auth[@]}" -H 'content-type: application/json'
         + -d "$body" "$URL" | jq -r .id) || {                           
      19 +  echo "OpsSwipe approval unavailable, so this command was not 
         +run." >&2; exit 2; }                                           
      20 +                                                               
      21 +for _ in $(seq 200); do # ~10 minutes                          
      22 +  status=$(curl -fsS "${auth[@]}" "$URL?id=$id" | jq -r .status
         + || echo pending)                                              
      23 +  case "$status" in                                            
      24 +    approved) exit 0 ;;                                        
      25 +    declined) echo "The human declined this command on their ph
         +one (OpsSwipe). Don't retry it; ask them what to do instead." >
         +&2; exit 2 ;;                                                  
      26 +  esac                                                         
      27 +  sleep 3                                                      
      28 +done                                                           
      29 +echo "No answer on OpsSwipe within 10 minutes, so this command 
         +was not run." >&2                                              
      30 +exit 2                                                         
  ⎿  Created ~/storage/Files/opsswipe/integrations/claude-code/README.md
     (+24 -0)
       1 +# OpsSwipe gate for Claude Code                                
       2 +                                                               
       3 +Risky commands Claude Code wants to run (force-push, `db reset`
         +, `terraform apply`, …) wait for your                          
       4 +approval on your phone. Everything else runs as usual.         
       5 +                                                               
       6 +1. In OpsSwipe: **Settings → Agent access → New agent token**,

  changes haven't been seen on a phone yet: the new screens need the dev server

  The icon and the two screens

  - Icon: the back card was nearly black on a black background, so it
    disappeared, and the mark sat off-centre past Android's safe zone. It's now
    centred, the back card has a green outline, and it fits inside the launcher
    mask. One script (infra/icon.py) draws every size, so they stay consistent.
  - Welcome: the blurry picture in a black box is replaced by the real card,
    drawn by the app. It shows a fix that's ready and proven. The three slides
    now tell the new story:
    a. it breaks, and your phone knows first;
    b. the AI writes the fix and CI proves it;
    c. you approve with a swipe and your fingerprint, and get a postmortem.

    The name now reads "OpsSwipe" everywhere.
  - Create account: the empty lower half now has a reassurance line ("Nothing
    runs on your servers until you approve it with your fingerprint…") and a
    Privacy link.

  New features

  Feature: Fix ready before you wake up (the "wtf" one)
  What people see: Push: "checkout-api is down. A fix is ready and proven. 3/3
  requests pass. Swipe to merge." The AI fix, its test and the CI proof all
  happen before you've looked at your phone
  Plan: Pro, switched on per service
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: AI agent gate
  What people see: An agent asks to run a command (force-push, db reset,
  terraform apply). The card shows the exact command, and it only runs once you
   approve. Includes a ready-made hook for Claude Code that blocks the command
  when no answer comes
  Plan: Pro
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Feature: Public status page
  What people see: One link showing whether each service is up, 90 days of
  uptime
  and past incidents. It shows service names only, never errors or request

❯ Ok now i have one issue in onboarding we don't ask for signup or enable       
  fingerprint or things like this and what other thing you see missing.         

● Let me check exactly what the first-run flow does today before listing gaps.

  Read 1 file, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)
  ⎿  Loaded CLAUDE.md
  ⎿  Loaded AGENTS.md

● Sign-up is already in the flow. Today it's: Welcome (3 slides) → Create 
  account (GitHub or email) → Turn on alerts → you land on an empty Incidents
  tab, with a setup checklist under Services. If you didn't see Create account,
  you were probably still signed in from before; the purge will show it.

  Your other point is real: the fingerprint is never set up or tried during 
  onboarding. The first time the app asks for it is during a real outage. If the
  phone has no screen lock, you find out at 3am that you can't approve
  anything.

  What's missing from onboarding

  1. Fingerprint setup and a practice swipe. Right after alerts, show a sample
     card: "Swipe to fix, then confirm with your fingerprint." That checks the
     phone has a fingerprint or screen lock, sends people to phone settings if
     it doesn't, and teaches the swipe before a real outage. The sample incident
     already exists; it's just hidden behind a button on the empty screen.
  2. "Where does your app run?" Offer Render, Railway, a Google Cloud VM or
     Other, plus "Solo or team?". This tailors the next step, so people go
     straight to the right connect flow instead of an empty tab. The "team"
     answer also leads to the invite later.
  3. Connecting the first service inside onboarding. Today people are dropped on
     "Not watching anything yet" and have to find the checklist under Services.
     Walk them through GitHub → their host → pick the service, with Skip on
     every step. This is the step that decides whether they ever get value.
  4. A Pro trial offer at the end. Subscription apps normally show the offer
     once people understand the value, and RevenueCat use is one of the judging
     criteria. Today the paywall only appears when an outage hits the free
     limit, which is the worst moment to meet it for the first time. Make it
     "Start 7-day Pro trial" with a clear "Not now".
  5. Alerts that get through. Android can delay or silence alerts through
     battery optimisation and Do Not Disturb. A pager needs "Allow OpsSwipe in
     Do Not Disturb" and "Don't optimise battery", asked alongside the alerts
     permission.
  6. Smaller gaps:
     - Onboarding asks for no name or workspace, so status pages and weekly
       reports don't know what to call you.
     - There's no "Invite your teammate" step for people who chose Team.
     - The progress dots only cover the 3 slides. Setup steps should show "Step
       2 of 4".

  Proposed flow

  Welcome → Create account → Where does your app run? Solo or team? → Alerts
  (plus Do Not Disturb and battery) → Secure your fixes: a practice swipe with a
  real fingerprint → Connect your first service (skippable) → Pro trial
  (skippable) → Incidents, where your service shows as "Watching".

  It's about 3–4 hours. Items 1, 3 and 4 matter most for the demo video and the
  judges; I'd build those first, then 2 and 5. Every step can be skipped, and
  returning users who sign in skip all of it.

  Build it this way, or change anything first?

● What rows 16 and 17 are

  - 16. Pricing tiers in RevenueCat. Today RevenueCat has a single pro
    entitlement, one offering and one paywall. This row means creating the real
    plans in RevenueCat: products for each tier, entitlements for what each
    unlocks, an offering the paywall shows, and a free trial. The server then
    checks which tier you're on, not just "Pro or not". You create the products
    in the RevenueCat dashboard, and I wire up the entitlements, paywall and
    server checks.
  - 17. Indie-dev positioning. Rewriting the words, not the code: the README
    headline, the onboarding slides, the Devpost text and the video script, all
    aimed at "solo dev or small team whose app makes money". Pure copywriting,
    about an hour.

  What users complain about in existing tools

  From incident.io's interviews with 150+ PagerDuty customers and recent
  surveys:

  Complaint: Noise: paged for things that don't need them
  Evidence: "~90% of overnight alerts were not critical"; "20 to 100 alerts for
  a
  single outage" (incident.io). About 50 alerts a week per engineer, and only
  2–5% need a human (PagerDuty 2025, via OneUptime). 57% of teams say under 30%
   of alerts are actionable (2026 report, via  Carrier Management)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Complaint: Expensive, with add-ons and per-seat minimums
  Evidence: "$3,000 a month… not worth it"; a 60% price increase "made us feel
  held hostage"; status pages and AI are paid extras
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Complaint: Everything after "acknowledge" is manual
  Evidence: "After acknowledgment, everything that happens after that is more or

  less manual"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Complaint: Burnout
  Evidence: Nearly 70% of SREs say on-call stress drives burnout and people
  leaving (Catchpoint 2025, via Uptime Labs)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Complaint: Weeks of setup, outdated interface
  Evidence: "Configuration is a mess"; "Really outdated UI that engineers hate
  using"

  OpsSwipe already answers the manual part (the fix is ready and proven, you
  swipe) and the price part (flat pricing, no add-ons).

  The big thing that's missing

  OpsSwipe only hears from sources it built itself: your app's own error
  reports, its health check and Sentry. Real teams already run Datadog, Grafana
  or Prometheus alerting, UptimeRobot, or PagerDuty and Opsgenie integrations.
  They can't switch to OpsSwipe without rewiring everything, and when a real
  outage fires 20–100 alerts, there's nothing to collapse them into one card.

  Opsgenie shuts down for good on April 5, 2027: no new sales since June 2025,
  the API goes dark, and unmigrated data is deleted (Hyperping, AlertOps). Every
  Opsgenie team has to move somewhere within about 18 months.

  So the big feature is an alert inbox that only wakes you when there's 
  something to fix:
  1. One webhook URL per account that accepts Alertmanager, Grafana, Datadog,
     UptimeRobot, Opsgenie-style and plain JSON alerts. You point your existing
     tools at it, with no code changes.
  2. Collapse duplicates: 50 alerts about the same service become one card, with
     an "×50" counter.
  3. Decide what's worth waking for. An alert that matches a service OpsSwipe
     can fix (or a real failing request) wakes you with a fix. Everything else
     goes into a quiet inbox, summarised in the morning and in the weekly
     report. That turns the "2–5% need a human" statistic into the pitch.

  The line for the video: "Other pagers wake you 50 times a week. 2–5% of those 
  need you. OpsSwipe only wakes you with the fix in hand."

  It's about 4–5 hours. It reuses the existing incident pipeline, Sentry-style
  webhook secrets, and the weekly report.

  What competitors charge (2026)

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Product     │                         Price                         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ $21 (Professional) / $41 (Business) per user/mo on    │
  │ PagerDuty       │ annual billing, 5-user minimum; $25 / $49             │

  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Better Stack    │ $29/mo per responder, plus $12 per extra status page  │
  │                 │ (Hyperping)                                           │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sentry Seer (AI │ $40 per active contributor/mo (Sentry)                │
  │  fixes)         │                                                       │

  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

  │ (status pages)  │                                                       │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  My earlier Indie $9 was too cheap for what it includes. Revised proposal:
  - Free: 1 service, the first outage free, the status page and the weekly
    report.
  - Solo, $15/mo flat: unlimited services and fixes, AI fixes, "fix ready before
    you wake up", and the alert inbox. That's cheaper than Better Stack ($29)
    or Seer ($40) alone, while doing both jobs.
  - Team, $12/user/mo, minimum 2: adds escalation, the agent gate, and team
    members. That's about half of PagerDuty Professional, with no 5-seat minimum
    and no add-ons.

  Proposed build order

  1. The onboarding rework from my last message (fingerprint practice, first
     service, trial).
  2. The alert inbox and noise filter (the big missing piece).
  3. Pricing tiers in RevenueCat, plus the positioning rewrite (rows 16 and 17).

  Tell me to go ahead in that order, or reorder it.

  What this round found:
  - Reviews support the "fix, not just alert" pitch.
    - PagerDuty's app: slow acknowledgments, phone calls that keep coming after
      you've acknowledged, and 3+ weeks for a support reply.
    - incident.io on G2: its mobile experience is "less developed".
    - UptimeRobot on r/sysadmin: one user got 140 emails about services going up
      and down while everything was fine.
  - A second tool is gone. Grafana OnCall's open-source version was archived on
    March 24, 2026 and its APIs stopped working. So both Opsgenie (April 2027)
    and Grafana OnCall users need a new home.
  - The agent gate is timely. In 2025–26, AI agents deleted production data
    several times:
    - Replit wiped the SaaStr founder's database.
    - A coding agent deleted a company's database and all its backups in 9
      seconds.
    - A Claude Code Prisma migration emptied a live Supabase database.

    The security write-ups all conclude that a prompt isn't a control and risky
    actions need an approval step before they run. Cleric, an AI-SRE startup,
    even sells "read-only, human approves" as a feature, while Resolve AI raised
    at a $1.5B valuation for AI SRE.
  - Squadcast costs $12/user after a free tier of up to 5 users. That makes it
    the cheapest pager, so Team at $12/user competes on what we do (fixes,
    proof), not on price.
  - Downtime costs vary 10× between sources (roughly $300–$1,670 a minute for
    small companies). That's why showing the owner's own revenue at risk from
    RevenueCat is more convincing than any survey figure.

  Revised pricing:
  - Free: 1 service, first outage free, status page, weekly report.
  - Solo, $15/mo flat: replaces Better Stack plus Sentry Seer, which cost $69
    together.
  - Team, $12/user, minimum 2: versus PagerDuty at $21–49 per user with a 5-seat
    minimum.

  Sign-up is already in the flow. Today it's: Welcome (3 slides) → Create       
  account (GitHub or email) → Turn on alerts → you land on an empty Incidents   
  tab, with a setup checklist under Services. If you didn't see Create account, 
  you were probably still signed in from before; the purge will show it.        
                                                                                
  Your other point is real: the fingerprint is never set up or tried during     
  onboarding. The first time the app asks for it is during a real outage. If    
  the phone has no screen lock, you find out at 3am that you can't approve      
  anything.                                                                     
                                                                                
  What's missing from onboarding                                                
                                                                                
  1. Fingerprint setup and a practice swipe. Right after alerts, show a sample  
  card: "Swipe to fix, then confirm with your fingerprint." That checks the     
  phone has a fingerprint or screen lock, sends people to phone settings if it  
  doesn't, and teaches the swipe before a real outage. The sample incident      
  already exists; it's just hidden behind a button on the empty screen.         
  2. "Where does your app run?" Offer Render, Railway, a Google Cloud VM or     
  Other, plus "Solo or team?". This tailors the next step, so people go         
  straight to the right connect flow instead of an empty tab. The "team" answer 
  also leads to the invite later.                                               
  3. Connecting the first service inside onboarding. Today people are dropped   
  on "Not watching anything yet" and have to find the checklist under Services. 
  Walk them through GitHub → their host → pick the service, with Skip on every  
  step. This is the step that decides whether they ever get value.              
  4. A Pro trial offer at the end. Subscription apps normally show the offer    
  once people understand the value, and RevenueCat use is one of the judging    
  criteria. Today the paywall only appears when an outage hits the free limit,  
  which is the worst moment to meet it for the first time. Make it "Start 7-day 
  Pro trial" with a clear "Not now".                                            
  5. Alerts that get through. Android can delay or silence alerts through       
  battery optimisation and Do Not Disturb. A pager needs "Allow OpsSwipe in Do  
  Not Disturb" and "Don't optimise battery", asked alongside the alerts         
  permission.

● I audited everything built today: the backend, the three AI prompts and the
  app screens. I fixed and deployed 11 issues (commit efc597d). All 86 backend
  tests pass, the app tests, lint and typecheck are clean, and the
  fix-suggestion eval still scores 100%. The one thing I couldn't check is how
  the screens actually look, because I can't see the phone.

  What I found and fixed

  Area: Proof security (the most serious)
  Problem: The CI job that proves a fix also ran the PR's own code while it had
  access to the token that signs the proof. A fix steered by a malicious
  request could have posted a fake "3/3 passed"
  Fix: The PR's code now runs with the token variables removed; only OpsSwipe's
  own reporting step can sign
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Prompts (all 3)
  Problem: None said the failing requests and commit messages come from outside
  and may contain planted instructions
  Fix: Each prompt now says to treat them as data, never instructions
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Prompts
  Problem: The regression test was allowed to call the network
  Fix: It now must use only the app it starts and your normal test command
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Prompts
  Problem: "Claude" was named, but NVIDIA runs the AI
  Fix: Neutral wording
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Fix ready before you wake up
  Problem: It would have opened a pointless AI PR for a stopped VM, which isn't
  a
  code bug
  Fix: Only runs when a known release broke things
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Automatic PR descriptions
  Problem: They falsely said "opened after the on-call engineer approved it"
  Fix: Now say they were opened automatically and nothing merges until you
  approve
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Postmortems
  Problem: They would be written for false alarms, declined agent proposals and
  command approvals
  Fix: Only real outages get one
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Escalation
  Problem: Re-checked already-escalated incidents every minute
  Fix: Filtered out in the query
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Teams
  Problem: No member limit, despite the "up to 10" plan
  Fix: Capped at 10
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Wording
  Problem: "$4.99/month" in two paywall messages (the price changed); "Claude"
  on
  cards; Railway missing from setup text
  Fix: Now 7-day trial wording, "Rules + AI", and Railway listed
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Area: Slack sign-in
  Problem: Blocked the blank helper pages Slack uses internally
  Fix: Allowed

  Deliberately left

  - Proof isolation still has a ceiling. Code in the same CI job could, with
    real effort, read the runner's memory. Full isolation needs a separate job
    or container; it's noted in the code, and after the hackathon.
  - The demo repo's copy of the proof workflow needs the same token fix. I'm
    holding it until after Outage 2: any push to the demo's main now would break
    the revert you're about to test.

  Screens: what I can and can't check

  From the code, the layout is consistent: every colour comes from the theme,
  and there are only a handful of one-off font sizes, all deliberate. Alignment
  and crowding can only be judged on the device. Settings in particular has
  grown long:
  - Account
  - Plan
  - Alerts
  - Slack/Discord
  - Alert inbox
  - Team
  - Status page
  - Agent access
  - About

  Once the new build is installed, send screenshots of Welcome → the fingerprint
  practice → the trial → Services → Settings (scrolled) → an incident's detail.
  They come through now, and I'll fix spacing and grouping from what I see.

● Today's prompts are solid on content but fall short in three ways: raw
  unescaped JSON input instead of labelled tags, flat rule lists instead of a
  structured format, and no context on who consumes the output. I'll rewrite all
  three prompts with escaped, tagged input and the standard Role → Context →
  Input → Task → Constraints → Output structure.

  Both items you asked for

  1. Proof isolation is now fully closed. The PR's own code (install, tests, the
     app) runs inside Docker containers on copies of the repo. It can't see the
     CI machine's memory or environment, can't get the token that signs the
     proof, and can't touch the reporting script. I tested it with a throwaway
     PR on the demo repo: the run passed, then I closed the PR and deleted its
     branch.

     so /api/price is healthy again. The pending Outage 2 revert no longer
     works as a result. Today's old card closes on its own after 10 quiet
     minutes, and Outage 2 gets tested fresh in the walkthrough after the purge.

  Prompts: professional now?

  Yes. The three prompts live in supabase/functions/_shared/: suggest.ts (picks
  the fix), patch.ts (writes the AI fix) and postmortem.ts. They now follow the
  standard structure: Role, what happens to the answer, Input, Rules, Output,
  and what to do when unsure.

  The input arrives as labelled data (<incident>…</incident>), escaped so text
  from a request can't close the tag and pose as instructions.

  With the new triage prompt, NVIDIA got 10 of 10 of its own answers right, up
  from 93%. Four cases fell back to the rules because NVIDIA's API was
  overloaded.

  On f""" """: that's Python syntax, and this code is TypeScript. The TypeScript
  equivalent is a multi-line template string in backticks, which is what the
  prompts already use. They read cleanly, for example:
  const SYSTEM = `# Role
  You are the fix writer in OpsSwipe. A commit broke production; ...

  # Rules
  - Change only files from the list you were given ...`;
  Values are inserted with ${...} inside the backticks, the same idea as {...}
  in an f-string. No change is needed.

● No, don't switch to f""" """. It only exists in Python, and this backend is
  TypeScript (Deno), where an f-string is a syntax error and won't run. The
  TypeScript equivalent is the backtick template string the prompts already use:
  multi-line, with ${value} for inserting values. They're already in the clean
  form you want.

❯ shouldn't we have monthly yearly both                                         

● Yes, offer both. It's standard for subscription apps, and it makes the
  RevenueCat use look more thoughtful to the judges (a monthly/yearly toggle
  plus a trial). Yearly also brings money in upfront and is chosen by a large
  share of subscribers when the discount is clear.

  Proposed prices:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │          Plan          │ Monthly │ Yearly  │ Yearly works out to │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Pro                    │ $14.99  │ $119.99 │ ~$10/mo (save 33%)  │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ Team (up to 10 people) │ $39.99  │ $359.99 │ ~$30/mo (save 25%)  │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┴─────────────────────┘

  - Put the 7-day free trial on the yearly plans (optionally on monthly too). A
    trial on the yearly plan is the common way to steer people to it.
  - Show "Save 33%" or "$10/mo" on the yearly option, pre-selected.

  No code changes are needed. The server and app only check the pro and team
  entitlements, never product IDs or durations. RevenueCat's paywall shows
  whatever packages the offering contains.

  The updated dashboard list:
  1. Products (4):
     - opsswipe_pro_monthly
     - opsswipe_pro_yearly
     - opsswipe_team_monthly
     - opsswipe_team_yearly

     Each gets the prices above, and the 7-day trial on at least the yearly
     ones.
  2. Entitlements:
     - pro: attach all 4 products.
     - team: attach the 2 Team products.
  3. Offering default, 4 packages:
     - Monthly → Pro monthly
     - Annual → Pro yearly
     - custom team_monthly → Team monthly
     - custom team_annual → Team yearly
  4. Paywall: use a multi-tier template (Pro | Team tabs) with a Monthly/Yearly
     toggle. Yearly is selected by default with its savings badge, and the trial
     is shown.

  One wording tweak so nothing sounds misleading: the onboarding screen says
  "Try Pro free for 7 days". That stays true as long as the Pro plans have the
  trial.

  Our closest competitors (none flagged as student)

  Project: Store Medic
  What it does: AI agents watch a WooCommerce store; "Watch → Verify → Approve";

  one tap runs the real fix
  vs OpsSwipe: The closest idea, but only for WooCommerce stores, with no code
  fixes and no proof
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Project: Notiformer
  What it does: A human-in-the-loop API: an AI agent pauses and asks your phone
  to approve
  vs OpsSwipe: The same idea as our agent gate. They're only the gate; ours sits

  inside a full outage loop and ships with a Claude Code hook
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Project: Hangar
  What it does: Buttons on the phone that fire deploy and rollback webhooks
  vs OpsSwipe: Manual buttons: nothing detects the outage or suggests a fix, and

  there's no AI or proof
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Project: Avengents AI, Macon+
  What it does: Control Claude Code or Codex from your phone
  vs OpsSwipe: About coding sessions, not production
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Project: MRR Alerts, AppGlance
  What it does: Revenue and analytics alerts for indie devs
  vs OpsSwipe: Overlaps our "revenue at risk", but they only show numbers; they
  don't fix anything
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Project: Actuator, Pulse
  What it does: Server metrics on the phone
  vs OpsSwipe: Monitoring only

  Where OpsSwipe stands

  - Unique: no other entry proves a fix by replaying the real failed production
    requests in CI before merge, or writes the fix before you wake up. That's
    the originality claim, and it holds against all 1,964 entries.
  - Watch out:
    - Store Medic's "nothing changes without your approval" and Notiformer's 
      one-line agent approval will read similar to a quick judge. Our video must
      show what they can't: the proof ("3/3 failing requests now pass") and the
      AI-written fix with its test.
    - Among students we're rare: likely the only production-ops product among
      ~51 clear student entries. It helps us stand out, but only if the first 10
      seconds tell an indie-dev story, since most judges build consumer apps.

  # Plan v7: Sep 29 afternoon

  Everything below is built, tested and deployed on `app-v2-accounts-ai-agents`
  (commits `f1b6e73` to `72c33b1`). 86 backend tests, app tests, lint, typecheck
  and CI all green.

  ## Built this afternoon

  | Area | What it does | Plan |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Onboarding | After alerts: (1) a practice card approved with the phone's
  real fingerprint, or a nudge to set up a screen lock if the phone has none;
  (2) "Try Pro free for 7 days"; (3) Services opens with the add-a-service
  sheet. Every step skippable; returning users skip it | - |
  | Pro and Team plans | Pro unlocks the `pro` entitlement, Team unlocks `team`
  and includes everything in Pro. Team invites need Team. Settings shows Free /
  Pro / Team | - |
  | Alert inbox | One private webhook URL for Grafana, Alertmanager or any JSON.
  50 alerts about one service become one card ("x50 alerts, one card"); the
  rest stay quiet and are counted in the weekly report. `chaos.sh noise` fires a
  50-alert burst for the demo | Pro |
  | New paywall | Pro / Team switcher, Monthly / Yearly (yearly default), "Start
  7-day free trial" | - |

  ## Pricing (RevenueCat, Test Store)

  | Plan | Monthly | Yearly | Includes |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | Free | $0 | - | 1 service, first outage free, status page, weekly report |
  | Pro | $14.99 | $119.99 (save 33%) | Unlimited outages, AI fixes with
  regression tests, fix ready before you wake up, alert inbox, agent approvals |
  | Team (up to 10) | $39.99 | $359.99 (save 25%) | Everything in Pro,
  teammates, 5-minute escalation |

  All four products have a 7-day free trial. Offering `default`: `$rc_monthly`,
  `$rc_annual`, `team_monthly`, `team_annual`.

  ## Audit fixes

  | Issue | Fix |
  | --- | --- |
  | The CI job proving a fix also ran the PR's own code while it could reach the
  proof-signing token | The PR's install, tests and app now run in Docker
  containers on copies of the repo: no access to the runner's memory,
  environment, token or reporter. Verified on a throwaway PR in the demo repo |
  | Prompts did not say request data is untrusted | All three prompts treat
  request paths, bodies and commit messages as data, never instructions |
  | "Fix ready before you wake up" would open an AI PR for a stopped VM | Only
  when a known release broke it |
  | Automatic AI PRs claimed "approved by the engineer" | They now say they were
  opened automatically |
  | Postmortems written for false alarms, declined proposals and command
  approvals | Only real outages get one |
  | Escalation re-checked escalated incidents every minute; teams had no size
  limit | Filtered in the query; teams capped at 10 |
  | Stale copy: "$4.99", "Claude" on cards, Railway missing | Trial wording,
  "Rules + AI", Railway listed |

  ## Prompts

  The three prompts (`_shared/suggest.ts`, `patch.ts`, `postmortem.ts`) now
  follow Role, what happens to the answer, Input, Rules, Output, and what to do
  when unsure. Input arrives as `<incident>JSON</incident>` with "<" escaped, so
  text from a request cannot close the tag and pose as instructions. NVIDIA
  eval with the new triage prompt: 10 of 10 of its own answers correct (was
  93%); 4 cases fell back to rules when NVIDIA was overloaded.

  ## UI polish (taste audit)

  One plan name everywhere (Free / Pro / Team), one label for upgrading ("Try
  Pro free"), one radius token for icon badges, no en-dash placeholders, shorter
  alert-inbox copy.

  ## Competitors in the Shipaton gallery

  1,964 projects scraped (names, write-ups, built-with). Devpost does not show
  categories; 138 write-ups mention being a student (51 clearly), mostly AI
  assistants, productivity, games and health. About 13 student entries are
  developer tools; none do what OpsSwipe does.

  | Project | What it does | vs OpsSwipe |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Store Medic | AI agents watch a WooCommerce store; Watch, Verify, Approve;
  one tap runs the fix | Closest idea, stores only, no code fixes, no proof |
  | Notiformer | Human-in-the-loop API: an AI agent pauses and asks your phone |
  Same idea as our agent gate; ours sits inside a full outage loop, with a
  Claude Code hook |
  | Hangar | Phone buttons that fire deploy and rollback webhooks | Manual; no
  detection, AI or proof |
  | Avengents AI, Macon+ | Control coding agents from the phone | About coding
  sessions, not production |
  | MRR Alerts, AppGlance | Revenue and analytics alerts for indie devs |
  Overlaps "revenue at risk"; they do not fix |

  Unique to OpsSwipe: proving a fix by replaying the real failed production
  requests in CI, and writing the fix before you wake up. The video must show
  the proof ("3/3 failing requests now pass") so we don't look like Store Medic
  or Notiformer.

  ## Next

  1. EAS build (new icon, in-app Slack sign-in, onboarding): `cd app && npx
  eas-cli build --profile development --platform android`
  2. Purge, then a full walkthrough from a fresh install: onboarding, Outage 1
  (reboot), Outage 2 with Fix with AI (diff, proof, merge, postmortem),
  `chaos.sh noise`, Claude Code asking the phone, Discord, status page.
  Screenshots for layout fixes.
  3. Positioning copy for README, Devpost and the video script.
  4. Video: scrcpy clips, Remotion motion graphics, AI voice-over, 1:55.
  5. Merge to `main`, repo public, Devpost.

  When you're back on the owner account at 11 AM, I can also update the older
  tabs (the Master plan tracker and the Plan v6 statuses).

● Fetch(https://docs.railway.com/reference/pricing/free-trial)

● Yes. Railway's trial needs no card, according to its current docs. You get a
  one-time $5 credit for 30 days, with Hobby-plan features limited to 1 GB of
  RAM and 5 services per project (Railway free trial docs). Some third-party
  sites still say a card is required; that's an older rule, and Railway's own
  docs are what count.

  Two things matter for OpsSwipe:
  1. Connect GitHub when you sign up. Without a verified GitHub account you get
     a "Limited Trial" with restricted outbound network access. The demo app has
     to send its 5xx reports out to OpsSwipe, so you need the Full Trial. If
     Railway puts you on Limited, you can upgrade at railway.com/verify.
  2. The API token: create one under Account Settings → Tokens (an account
     token), then in OpsSwipe go to Services → Railway, paste the token, and
     paste each service's dashboard link. I'm fairly sure trial accounts can
     create tokens, but Railway's trial page doesn't say so explicitly. If the
     tokens page is locked, tell me.

  Limits to keep in mind: services pause after 30 days or once the $5 is used,
  whichever comes first. That's plenty for recording the video.

  Do you need it? Not for the demo. The Google Cloud VM already covers the whole
  story: reboot, revert or AI fix, proof, merge. Railway only helps if you want
  to show a restart or rollback on a second provider. I'd skip it unless
  there's spare time after the walkthrough.

  to Vercel and Fly. It fits the pattern we already use, so it's a small job

  the write-up says the builder is one.

  Only 235 of the 1,964 entries link a GitHub repo, and 220 of those are 
  reachable. A public, well-tested repo is itself a differentiator.

  Our closest competitors are thin underneath:
  - Store Medic: 13 commits, no tests, no CI, started Sep 18.
  - BlastRadius: 6 commits.
  - Meridian is big (2,957 commits, 378 stars), but it's a Rust project started
    in May and not a student entry. It's a different league of project, not our
    rival.
  - Notiformer, Hangar, MRR Alerts and AppGlance link no code at all, only
    websites and store pages.

  The strongest student entries by code quality:

  ┌─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────┐

  └─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

  Bloom is the one to watch: a consumer app, polished, with RevenueCat and CI.
  That's exactly what these judges build.

  How we beat them

  1. Progress, shown and not just claimed. No student entry shows a live
     production fix proven by CI. Record the full Fix with AI loop on a real 
     phone. Nobody else can show "3/3 failing requests now pass", and it's our
     biggest edge.
  2. Make Care visible in 30 seconds. Judges skim repos. Make the repo public,
     with:
     - a CI badge;
     - a "For the judges" box at the top: 86 tests, SQL tenancy tests, the eval,
       proof isolation in containers, the security model;
     - the architecture diagram.

     Two-thirds of public repos don't have CI, and a judge will notice ours in
     seconds.
  3. Tell it as a consumer-style story, not DevOps. Bloom and Veta win on feel.
     Our first 10 seconds must feel like theirs: a student whose app makes
     money, a phone buzzing at dinner, what it's costing. We're the only 
     production-safety app among ~51 clear student entries, which helps only if
     it's instantly understandable.
  4. Make RevenueCat part of the product. Most entries only put a paywall in
     front of features. Ours reads the user's own RevenueCat to show revenue at
     risk, keeps working when RevenueCat is down (webhook fallback), gives the
     first outage free, and offers a trial. Put revenue at risk on screen.
  5. Design polish from your screenshots. Bloom and Veta will look beautiful, so
     the walkthrough screenshots matter. I'll fix spacing, sizes and wording
     from them.
  6. Say it was built with Claude Code, openly. Last year a winner was praised
     for building openly with AI tools. The repo shows the tests and evals
     behind it, which sets us apart from quick AI-built apps.
  7. A small extra for visibility: likes are low everywhere (the top entry
     has 28). A short build-in-public post with the video link on X or LinkedIn
     could put OpsSwipe among the most-liked entries.

  Still the one blocker: everything above depends on the build and the fresh
  walkthrough. Start the EAS build now if it isn't running.

  Should I add this repo comparison to the "Architecture and winning plan" tab?

      walkthrough, to show three hosts working.
  12. Before the video: merge to main, then make the repo public.

  How to test teams (works with one phone)

  1. As account A (your main account): Settings → Plan → Try Pro free → Team tab
     → buy it. It's the Test Store, so nothing is charged.
  2. A: Settings → Team → Invite a teammate and note the 8-character code.
  3. Sign out and create account B (a different email). B: Settings → Team → 
     join with the code.
  4. Stay signed in as B. From the laptop run ./infra/chaos.sh gcp; it breaks
     A's demo VM.
  5. Check, as B:
     - A's incident shows up in B's Incidents tab straight away.
     - After 5 minutes with no answer, B's phone gets "Escalated: opsswipe-demo
       is down…".
     - B can swipe and fix it with B's own fingerprint.
     - B's Services tab stays empty: teammates never see A's services or keys.
  6. Sign back in as A: Activity shows the fix and who ran it.
