       1 +# Security                                                     
       2 +                                                               
       3 +OpsSwipe can restart services, reboot machines and merge code, 
         +so security reports are the most welcome issues we get.        
       4 +                                                               
       5 +## Reporting a vulnerability                                   
       6 +                                                               
       7 +Please **don't open a public issue**. Use GitHub's private repo
         +rting instead: **Security → Report a vulnerability** on        
       8 +this repository. Include what you found, how to reproduce it an
         +d what an attacker could do. You'll get an answer within       
       9 +three days.                                                    
      10 +                                                               
      11 +## How OpsSwipe is built to be safe                            
      12 +                                                               
      13 +The full model is in the README under [Security model](README.m
         +d#security-model). In short:                                   
      14 +                                                               
      15 +- The phone sends only an incident id and a fix name; the serve
         +r decides what may run, from validated config.                 
      16 +- Every fix needs the phone's fingerprint or screen lock; nothi
         +ng reaches production without a person approving it.           
      17 +- Cloud keys and tokens live in Supabase Vault, are read only b
         +y server code and never reach the phone.                       
      18 +- Row-level security keeps each account's data to itself; teamm
         +ates see incidents, never services or keys.                    
      19 +- Each Google Cloud VM gets a custom role that can only read it
         +s status and reset it.                                         
      20 +- AI output is checked by code before use; prompts treat reques
         +t data as data, never instructions.                            
      21 +- Code fixes merge only after CI replays the real failing reque
         +sts. The PR's own code runs in Docker and can't reach the      
      22 +  token that signs the proof, which is bound to the exact commi
         +t OpsSwipe opened.                                             
      23 +- Failing requests are scrubbed and replayed only in CI, never 
         +against production.                                            
      24 +                                                               
      25 +## Supported versions                                          
      26 +                                                               
      27 +Only the latest `main` is supported.

      169  this entry uses a read-only v2 key, detects the project from i
           t when the key may read project configuration, and keeps
      170  the whole feature optional: nothing in setup depends on it. So
           urce:
      171  [RevenueCat OAuth](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/projects/oa
           uth-overview).
      172 +                                                              
      173 +## 26. The AI writes a regression test, at a path we choose   
      174 +A fix that passes the replay proves this outage is over; a tes
          +t keeps it over. So Fix with AI also writes one regression    
      175 +test, modelled on a test already in the repo. It may only crea
          +te a file at a path OpsSwipe picks (`opsswipe-<sha>.test.*`   
      176 +next to the repo's tests): it can't rewrite or weaken an exist
          +ing test, and a test with no code change is rejected.         
      177 +                                                              
      178 +## 27. The proof runs the PR's code in containers             
      179 +The proof workflow must run the PR's code (install scripts, te
          +sts, the app) and must also sign its result with an OIDC      
      180 +token. In one job, the PR's code could mint that token and sig
          +n a fake "3/3 passed". Now install, tests and the app run     
      181 +in Docker on copies of the repo: they can't see the runner's m
          +emory, environment or token, and can't touch the              
      182 +reporter, which runs from the untouched checkout. Verified on 
          +a throwaway PR in the demo repo.                              
      183 +                                                              
      184 +## 28. Prompts as specifications, input as escaped data       
      185 +The three prompts (triage, fix, postmortem) follow one structu
          +re: role, what happens to the answer, input, rules, output,   
      186 +what to do when unsure. Input arrives as `<tag>JSON</tag>` wit
          +h `<` escaped, so a request path or commit message can't      
      187 +close the tag and pose as instructions, and every prompt says 
          +request data is data. Code still checks every answer.         
      188 +With the new triage prompt, Nemotron answered 10 of 10 eval ca
          +ses correctly (was 93%).                                      
      189 +                                                              
      190 +## 29. Fix ready before you wake up, only for code bugs       
      191 +Writing and proving the fix before anyone looks is the moment 
          +people remember, and it only opens a pull request; merging    
      192 +still needs a swipe. It runs only when a known release broke t
          +he service: a stopped VM or a network blip isn't a code       
      193 +bug, and an AI PR for it would be noise. It's opt-in per servi
          +ce and part of Pro.                                           
      194 +                                                              
      195 +## 30. Teams through row-level security, never through shared 
          +keys                                                          
      196 +A teammate needs to see and fix the owner's incidents, not the
          + owner's cloud. Incident and audit policies allow the         
      197 +owner's team; services, connections and keys stay owner-only, 
          +and `execute` bills the owner's plan whoever swipes. A SQL    
      198 +test proves a teammate sees the incident and nothing else. Esc
          +alation pages the team once, after 5 unanswered minutes.      
      199 +                                                              
      200 +## 31. Flat plans, because stores can't sell seats            
      201 +Per-seat pricing is the norm for pagers, and users hate it. Ap
          +p-store subscriptions can't be priced per seat anyway, so     
      202 +Pro is one price and Team is one price for up to 10 people, mo
          +nthly or yearly, each with a 7-day trial. The trial is        
      203 +offered once in onboarding after a practice fix; the paywall o
          +therwise appears only when the free outage is used.           
      204 +                                                              
      205 +## 32. An alert inbox instead of more integrations            
      206 +Teams already run Grafana, Alertmanager and uptime monitors, a
          +nd a real outage fires dozens of alerts. One private          
      207 +webhook URL takes them all: an alert about a watched service j
          +oins that service's one card; everything else stays           
      208 +quiet and is counted in the weekly report. That turns "only 2 
          +to 5% of pages need a human" into the product.

    take. And copying a secret through chat again would just add another place
    it has leaked.

  The better route also takes care of rotation. Make fresh ones and put them
  straight into GitHub, so they never pass through me:

  1. New access token: supabase.com/dashboard/account/tokens → Generate new 
     token → name it github-deploy → copy it.
  2. New database password: Supabase → Project Settings → Database → Reset 
     database password → copy it. This also retires the old one that was pasted
     in chat.
  3. Set both as GitHub secrets. Run these here; each asks for the value, and
     the value goes straight to GitHub without passing through me:
  ! gh secret set SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN -R DevHusnainAi/opsswipe
  ! gh secret set SUPABASE_DB_PASSWORD -R DevHusnainAi/opsswipe
  4. Once they're set, tell me. I'll check that both names exist; GitHub only
     shows me the names, never the values.

  Resetting the database password doesn't break the live app: the functions
  connect through Supabase's own internal credentials, not this password.

● Close, but one capability is wrong. Your token has:
  - Project metadata and settings: fine (read)
  - Database access: fine (read-write, for migrations)
  - Secrets available to edge functions: remove this one

  Add Edge Functions (read-write) instead. That's what supabase functions deploy
  needs, and it's missing, so the deploy would fail. The secrets capability
  would let the token read and change your API keys (NVIDIA, GitHub App,
  Google…), which the deploy never needs. It's the riskiest thing to hand to CI.

  It should end up as:

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │          Capability           │   Access   │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Project metadata and settings │ Read       │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Database                      │ Read-write │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Edge Functions                │ Read-write │
  └───────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  Keep: name CI deploy, expires in 30 days, only the opsswipe project. It will
  still say "High risk"; any token that can write to a database and deploy code
  is labelled that way, and the 30-day expiry and single project limit it.

  Then generate it and set it straight into GitHub:

✻ Worked for 6s · done 3:50 PM

❯ from where to get db password in supabase                                     

● Supabase never shows an existing database password; you can only set a new
  one. That works in our favour, because the old one was pasted in chat and
  should be replaced anyway.

  1. Open supabase.com/dashboard and select the opsswipe project.
  2. Go to Project Settings (the gear icon, bottom left) → Database. In some
     versions it's Database (left sidebar) → Settings.
  3. Find Database password → Reset database password.
  4. Click Generate a password, copy it, and confirm with Reset password.
  5. Put it straight into GitHub:
  ! gh secret set SUPABASE_DB_PASSWORD -R DevHusnainAi/opsswipe
     and paste it when asked.

  Resetting it doesn't affect the live app. The functions and the app use
  Supabase's own internal credentials, and the Supabase CLI on this computer
  signs in with your login, not this password.

✻ Crunched for 8s · done 3:51 PM

❯ then how will you know password the pasword you are using will it not change  

● I don't use the database password at all, so resetting it changes nothing for
  me.
  - On this computer: the Supabase CLI signs in with your Supabase login (from
    supabase login), not the database password. When I ran migrations earlier,
    the CLI printed "Initialising login role…": it creates a temporary database
    login of its own each time.
  - Deploying functions only needs that login or an access token, never the
    database password.
  - The live app and functions use Supabase's internal keys, which a password
    reset doesn't touch.

  The only thing that uses the new password is the GitHub deploy workflow, and
  it reads it from the SUPABASE_DB_PASSWORD secret you're setting. You never
  have to tell me the value.

  If a command here ever does ask for the database password, it will stop and
  prompt. You'd type it yourself with !, so it still never goes through chat.

✻ Sautéed for 2m 16s · done 3:56 PM

❯ for EAS what tokens we need                                                   

● Just one: EXPO_TOKEN. Everything else the build needs is already stored on
  EAS:
  - the Android signing key, created on the first build;
  - the EXPO_PUBLIC_… values (Supabase URL and key, RevenueCat key) in EAS's
    preview environment;
  - the Firebase push key.

  Best: a robot token. It's a token that belongs to a limited bot account rather
  than to you, so it can't touch your account settings.
  1. expo.dev → your account devhusnainai → Settings → Access tokens.
  2. Under Robot users, click Add robot, name it github-actions, role Developer
     (enough to start builds, not to manage the account).
  3. Create a token for that robot and copy it.
  4. Put it into GitHub without it passing through chat:
  ! gh secret set EXPO_TOKEN -R DevHusnainAi/opsswipe

  If you don't see "Robot users", a normal personal access token from the same
  page works too.

  To use it: GitHub → opsswipe repo → Actions → eas-build → Run workflow (choose
  preview or development). Or push a tag, e.g. git tag v1.0.0 && git push 
  --tags. The build then queues on EAS, and the APK link appears on expo.dev.

  For tonight, you don't need the workflow: the npx eas-cli build … command from
  before uses your own login on this computer.
